    gout, hyperuricemia, nephrolithiasis
    General Information
    Hyperuricemia may occur in patients receiving thiazide diuretics. In predisposed individuals, frank gout or nephrolithiasis may be precipitated. Diuretic-induced acute gouty arthritis and nephrolithiasis are extremely rare in patients with no prior history of gout. If some manifestation of gout occurs in a patient who requires diuretic therapy for effective treatment of hypertension, allopurinol or a uricosuric agent can be given to prevent recurrent gouty attacks without diminishing the antihypertensive effect of benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide.

    General Information
    Thiazide diuretics have been shown to increase the urinary excretion of magnesium; this may result in hypomagnesemia. After approximately one year of treatment, total serum cholesterol concentrations will subside to baseline or lower, suggesting that diuretic therapy does not increase coronary heart disease risk. Nevertheless, it would be prudent to monitor serum cholesterol and triglyceride concentrations periodically in hypertensive patients receiving benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide.

    Headline
    teratogenesis
    General Information
    Due to the potential for teratogenesis, benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide should not be used during pregnancy. ACE inhibitors have been associated with fetal and neonatal abnormalities when administered to women during the second or third trimesters of pregnancy. Adverse fetal and neonatal effects have included hypotension, neonatal skull hypoplasia, anuria, renal failure, oligohydramnios, and death. Oligohydramnios has been associated with fetal lung hypoplasia and skeletal deformations. First trimester use of thiazide and related diuretics may increase the risk for congenital defects. In addition to malformations, other fetal risks associated with thiazide use during pregnancy include hypoglycemia, thrombocytopenia, hyponatremia, hypokalemia, and death from maternal complications. If oliguria or hypotension occurs in a neonate with a history of in utero exposure to benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide, blood pressure and renal perfusion support may be required, as well as exchange transfusion or dialysis to reverse hypotension and/or support decreased renal function.

    Headline
    blurred vision, myopia, ocular hypertension, xanthopsia
    General Information
    Benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide may result in ophthalmic adverse reactions. Hydrochlorothiazide, a sulfonamide, can cause an idiosyncratic reaction, resulting in transient myopia and acute angle-closure glaucoma. Symptoms include acute onset of decreased visual acuity or ocular pain and typically occur within hours to weeks of drug initiation. Untreated acute angle-closure glaucoma can lead to permanent vision loss. Discontinue hydrochlorothiazide as rapidly as possible and consider prompt medical or surgical treatment if the ocular hypertension remains uncontrolled. Transient blurred vision and xanthopsia have also been reported during hydrochlorothiazide monotherapy.

Description
  Text
  Benazepril and hydrochlorothiazide are used together in an oral preparation to treat hypertension. These ingredients have additive hypotensive activity. Hydrochlorothiazide is a thiazide diuretic. Benazepril is an angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitor. Benazepril can counteract the hypokalemia caused by hydrochlorothiazide. Benazepril is a prodrug that is hydrolyzed by esterases to the active metabolite benazeprilat, whose long duration of action makes benazepril suitable for once daily dosing. Pharmacodynamically, benazepril most closely resembles fosinopril, quinapril, and ramipril. Neither benazepril nor benazeprilat contain a sulfhydryl group, which has implications regarding the drug's adverse reactions profile. Although benazepril monotherapy is less effective in black hypertensive patients (a low-renin hypertensive population), efficacy with benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide (Lotensin HCT®) appears to be independent of race. Lotensin HCT® was granted FDA approval for the treatment of hypertension on May 19, 1992. Generic benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide was approved in February 2004.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  The effects of benazepril and hydrochlorothiazide on blood pressure are additive. Thiazide diuretics lower the blood pressure by increasing the excretion of sodium; whereas ACE inhibitors lower blood pressure by blocking the renin-angiotensin system. Benazepril can counteract the hypokalemia caused by the hydrochlorothiazide.
 
Hydrochlorothiazide: Thiazide diuretics increase the excretion of water by inhibiting the reabsorption of sodium and chloride ions at the distal renal tubule. The natriuretic effects are accompanied by a secondary loss of potassium and bicarbonate which can cause a mild hypokalemic, hypochloremic, metabolic alkalosis. Thiazides also decrease the elimination of calcium and uric acid. Thiazides diuretics usually do not affect normal blood pressure. When chronically administered, diuretics decrease peripheral vascular resistance. The exact mechanism responsible for the lowered peripheral resistance is not known; however, excretion of urinary sodium by the kidneys is required to achieve blood pressure reduction. Initially, diuretics lower blood pressure by decreasing cardiac output, plasma volume and extracellular fluid volume. Cardiac output eventually returns to normal, plasma and extracellular fluid values return to slightly less than normal, but peripheral vascular resistance is reduced, resulting in lower blood pressure. Thiazide diuretics also decrease the glomerular filtration rate, which contributes to the drug's lower efficacy in patients with renal impairment. The changes in plasma volume induce an elevation in plasma renin activity and aldosterone secretion which contributes to the potassium losses associated with thiazide diuretics. In general, diuretics can worsen glucose tolerance and lipid abnormalities.
 
Benazepril: Benazepril competes with angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) for the substrate angiotensin I, thereby blocking its conversion to angiotensin II. Angiotensin II is a potent vasoconstrictor and a negative-feedback mediator for renin activity. Thus, when benazepril lowers angiotensin II plasma levels, blood pressure decreases and plasma renin activity increases. In addition, baroreceptor reflex mechanisms are stimulated in response to the fall in blood pressure. ACE inhibition also blocks the conversion of bradykinin, a potent vasodilator, to inactive peptides. Whether increased bradykinin levels play a part in the therapeutic effects of ACE inhibitors is presently unclear. Bradykinin-induced vasodilation is thought to be of secondary importance in the blood-pressure lowering effect of ACE inhibitors. A bradykinin mechanism may, however, contribute to ACE-inhibitor-induced angioneurotic edema.
 
ACE-inhibiting drugs may act locally to reduce vascular tone by decreasing local angiotensin II-induced sympathetic and/or vasoconstrictive activity. Decreases in plasma angiotensin II levels also reduce aldosterone secretion, subsquently decreasing sodium and water retention.
 
Benazepril causes arterial dilation, thereby lowering total peripheral vascular resistance. In hypertensive patients, blood pressure is decreased, with little or no change in heart rate, stroke volume, or cardiac output. Both standing and supine blood pressure are reduced following administration of benazepril, and although symptomatic hypotension is rare, it could occur more often in patients who are hypovolemic or hyponatremic. As antihypertensives, ACE inhibitors reduce LVH, do not worsen insulin resistance, and do not cause sexual dysfunction.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Benazepril: Benazepril is absorbed rapidly from the GI tract. Food does not affect the extent of absorption. Benazepril is a prodrug; peak plasma concentrations of the active component, benazeprilat, are reached within 1—2 hours in the fasting state and within 2—4 hours if given with food. After oral administration, noticeable cardiovascular effects begin within 1 hour, with peak reductions occurring after 2—4 hours. 
Hydrochlorothiazide: Hydrochlorothiazide absorption from the GI tract varies depending on the formulation, dose, and presence of concomitant disease states. Absorption is reduced in patients with hepatic, cardiac, and/or renal disease. The bioavailability is approximately 60—70%. The onset of action is 2 hours following oral administration, with peak effects occurring at 4 hours. The duration of action ranges from 6—12 hours.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Benazepril: In patients with hepatic dysfunction due to cirrhosis, the serum levels of benazeprilat are essentially unchanged.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Benazepril: The disposition of benazepril and benazeprilat in patients with CrCl > 30 mL/min is similar to that in patients with normal renal function; however, in patients with CrCl < 30 mL/min, peak benazeprilat levels and the initial half-life (alpha phase) are increased. In patients with renal failure, biliary clearance may partially compensate for deficient renal clearance.
Hydrochlorothiazide: Results from a study evaluating the pharmacokinetics of hydrochlorothiazide in relation to renal function indicate the elimination half life increases with varying degrees of renal failure, from 6.4 hours for patients with normal renal function to 11.5 hours for patients with mild renal impairment (30—90 mL/min) to 20.7 hours in patients with CrCl < 30 mL/min.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    Hydrochlorothiazide: Limited data suggest the systemic clearance of hydrochlorothiazide is reduced in geriatric patients compared to younger patients.
  Text
  Benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide is administered orally. Concomitant administration of benazepril with hydrochlorothiazide has no effect on the bioavailability of either drug.
Benazepril: Hepatic metabolism of benazepril is required to generate the active metabolite benazeprilat, delaying maximum effects. The hypotensive effect of benazepril lasts approximately 24 hours, allowing for once-daily dosing. The drug is 95% plasma protein-bound and has a small volume of distribution. Parent drug is almost totally converted to benazeprilat, which is primarily renally eliminated, and, to a lesser extent (11—12%), by biliary excretion in patients with normal renal function. The effective half-life of benazeprilat is 10—11 hours.
Hydrochlorothiazide: Hydrochlorothiazide crosses the placenta, but not the blood-brain barrier, and is distributed into breast milk. Hydrochlorothiazide is not significantly metabolized and is excreted unchanged in the urine. At least 61% of the oral dose is eliminated unchanged within 24 hours. The elimination half-life ranges from 5.6—14.8 hours.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide, HCTZ should be used with caution in patients with hepatic disease since alterations of fluid and electrolyte balance may precipitate hepatic coma in some patients.
  Renal Impairment
  CrCl >= 30 mL/min: No dosage adjustment is necessary.
CrCl < 30 mL/min: The safety and efficacy of benazepril; hydrochlorothiazide have not been established in patients with severe renal impairment. In general, loop diuretics are preferred to thiazides in this population as thiazides are not effective in patients with severe renal impairment.
 
Intermittent hemodialysis
Combination therapy with hydrochlorothiazide is not recommended in patients with CrCl < 30 mL/min. Thiazide diuretics are not effective in this setting.
